绘制美国严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2、流感和呼吸道合胞病毒监测数据基础设施图谱

Mapping the Surveillance Data Infrastructure in the U.S. for SARS-CoV-2, Influenza, and Respiratory Syncytial Virus
作者： Akshay Deverakonda, Emily E. Ricotta, John T. Kubale
摘要中文翻译传染病监测构成公共卫生的流行病学基石,支撑健康数据的持续系统性收集与分析。然而,这些数据由碎片化的系统与实体网络收集,其可及性与高效利用受到复杂化影响。本综述描述了三种重要呼吸道病毒——流感、呼吸道合胞病毒(RSV)及严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2——的关键监测系统,突出这些系统的局限性,并识别改进机遇。高质量监测数据对有效公共卫生干预至关重要。美国公共卫生基础设施的分散性及由此形成的监测网络补丁式拼接,为试图动员这些数据用于疫情应对、政策评估或基于人群的研究的流行病学家带来了严峻挑战。明确这些关键监测系统、其收集的数据及所代表的人群,可支持其更有效地用于公共卫生研究与应急响应。我们识别出十三个在美国监测流感、RSV与严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2的监测数据源;除一个外均为政府系统。其中多数(9个)为基于病例的系统,2个为综合性监测系统(监测人群中与疾病相关症状增加而非疾病本身),2个被归类为“非常规”数据源,如废水监测系统。我们还评估了这些系统及常向其提供数据的州与地方实体的数据相对可及性,并汇编信息以提升其可发现性与可及性。在线版本包含补充材料,可于10.1007/s40471-026-00405-w访问。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
This review focuses on SARS-CoV-2, COVID-19, and named SARS-CoV-2 variants, examining the surveillance data infrastructure in the U.S. for these pathogens. The central question is to describe the key surveillance systems, highlight their limitations, and identify opportunities for improvement.
证据基础与方法
本综述为对十三个在美国监测流感、RSV与严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2的监测数据源的系统性分析。除一个外均为政府系统。方法包括评估这些系统数据的相对可及性,并汇编信息以提升其可发现性与可及性。
共识与结论
本综述识别出十三个美国严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2、流感与RSV监测数据源。研究强调多数系统为基于病例的系统,部分采用综合性或非常规方法(如废水监测)。这些系统收集的数据对公共卫生干预至关重要。
争议与缺口
美国公共卫生基础设施的分散性及监测网络的补丁式拼接,为流行病学家带来挑战。这些系统收集的数据及其代表的人群缺乏清晰度,阻碍其在疫情应对与基于人群的研究中的有效利用。
科研与实践启示
明确关键监测系统、其收集的数据及所代表的人群,可支持其更有效地用于公共卫生研究与应急响应。本综述还强调需改善数据可及性与可发现性。

Mapping the Surveillance Data Infrastructure in the U.S. for SARS-CoV-2, Influenza, and Respiratory Syncytial Virus

Authors: Akshay Deverakonda, Emily E. Ricotta, John T. Kubale
Original abstract (English)
Infectious disease surveillance forms the epidemiological bedrock of public health, supporting the continuous and systematic collection, and analysis of health data. However, these data are collected by a fragmented network of systems and entities which complicate their accessibility and efficient use. This review describes the key surveillance systems tracking three important respiratory viruses: influenza, respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), and SARS-CoV-2; highlights these systems' limitations; and identifies opportunities for improvement. High quality surveillance data are vital for effective public health interventions. The decentralized nature of the U.S. public health infrastructure and the subsequent patchwork of surveillance networks create a formidable challenge for epidemiologists seeking to mobilize these data for outbreak response, policy evaluation, or population-based research. Greater clarity on these key surveillance systems, the data they collect, and the populations they represent could support their more effective use to support public health research and emergency response. We identified thirteen surveillance data sources monitoring influenza, RSV, and SARS-CoV-2 in the U.S; all but one were governmental. Most (9) were case-based systems, 2 were syndromic surveillance systems (that monitor for increases in illness-associated symptoms in a population rather than the disease itself), and 2 were classified as "non-traditional" sources like wastewater surveillance systems. We also assessed the relative accessibility of data from these systems and of data from state and local entities which often feed into these systems, and collated this information to improve its findability and accessibility. The online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1007/s40471-026-00405-w.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This review focuses on SARS-CoV-2, COVID-19, and named SARS-CoV-2 variants, examining the surveillance data infrastructure in the U.S. for these pathogens. The central question is to describe the key surveillance systems, highlight their limitations, and identify opportunities for improvement.
Evidence base and method
The review is a systematic analysis of thirteen surveillance data sources monitoring influenza, RSV, and SARS-CoV-2 in the U.S. All but one were governmental. The methods involved assessing the relative accessibility of data from these systems and collating information to improve its findability and accessibility.
Consensus and conclusions
The review identifies thirteen surveillance data sources for SARS-CoV-2, influenza, and RSV in the U.S. It highlights that most systems are case-based, with some using syndromic or non-traditional methods like wastewater surveillance. The data collected by these systems are essential for public health interventions.
Controversies and gaps
The decentralized nature of the U.S. public health infrastructure and the patchwork of surveillance networks create challenges for epidemiologists. There is a lack of clarity on the data collected by these systems and the populations they represent, which hinders their effective use for outbreak response and population-based research.
Research and practice implications
Greater clarity on the key surveillance systems, the data they collect, and the populations they represent could support their more effective use for public health research and emergency response. The review also emphasizes the need for improved data accessibility and findability.

Association between psychological wellbeing and the COVID-19-related outcomes in Europeans aged ≥ 50 years old: The SHARE study.

Association between psychological wellbeing and the COVID-19-related outcomes in Europeans aged ≥ 50 years old: The SHARE study.
作者： Yumeng Gao, Nannan Wu, Suwen Shen, Xiaowei Zheng, Yujun Chen, Chao Shi
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
The study aimed to prospectively investigate the associations between psychological wellbeing and COVID-19 outcomes (vaccination, infection, long COVID) among Europeans aged ≥ 50 years old, using data from the Survey of Health, Aging, and Retirement in Europe (SHARE), specifically Wave 8 and two supplementary COVID-19 surveys (Corona Survey 1 and 2) covering 27 European countries and Israel.
证据基础与方法
本研究共纳入29,597名参与者。心理健康状况采用控制、自主、自我实现与愉悦感(CASP)-12量表进行评估。采用多变量 logistic 回归模型探索相关关联,并估计比值比(OR)及其95%置信区间(CI)。
共识与结论
Higher psychological wellbeing was associated with increased odds of receiving a COVID-19 vaccination (OR 2.68, 95% CI: 2.44–2.96) and a significant 23% reduction in the likelihood of long COVID (OR 0.77, 95% CI: 0.64–0.93). However, no significant association was found between psychological wellbeing and the risk of SARS-CoV-2 infection (OR 0.87, 95% CI: 0.74–1.02). Protective effects were more pronounced with higher CASP-12 scores.
争议与缺口
研究未观察到心理健康状况与严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染风险之间存在显著关联。作者强调有必要开展更高质量的研究以验证这些发现。
科研与实践启示
Further high-quality studies are warranted to confirm the findings and explore the mechanisms underlying the associations between psychological wellbeing and COVID-19 outcomes.

Association between psychological wellbeing and the COVID-19-related outcomes in Europeans aged ≥ 50 years old: The SHARE study.

Authors: Yumeng Gao, Nannan Wu, Suwen Shen, Xiaowei Zheng, Yujun Chen, Chao Shi
Original abstract (English)
ABSTRACT The aim of the present study was to prospectively investigate the associations between psychological wellbeing and COVID-19 outcomes (vaccination, infection, long COVID) based on the Survey of Health, Aging, and Retirement in Europe (SHARE), using the data from Wave 8 and two supplementary COVID-19 surveys (Corona Survey 1 and 2) covering 27 European countries and Israel. Furthermore, we also conducted a cross-sectional study to evaluated the joint effects of wellbeing and vaccination status on the risks of SARS-CoV-2 infection and long COVID. A total of 29,597 participants were included in this study. Psychological wellbeing was measured using the Control, Autonomy, Self-realization, and Pleasure (CASP)-12 scale. Multivariable logistic regression models were performed to explore these associations, and odds ratios (ORs) with 95% confidence intervals (CIs) were estimated. Compared with participants in the lowest CASP-12 score group, those in the highest group had significantly higher odds of COVID-19 vaccination (OR 2.68, 95% CI: 2.44–2.96) and a significant 23% reduction in the likelihood of long COVID (OR 0.77, 95% CI: 0.64–0.93). However, we failed to observe a significant association on infection risk (OR 0.87, 95% CI: 0.74–1.02). Protective effects were more pronounced with higher CASP-12 scores. Higher psychological wellbeing was associated with increased COVID-19 vaccination and reduced risks of infection and long COVID among European adults aged 50 y and older. Further high-quality studies were warranted to confirm these findings.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The study aimed to prospectively investigate the associations between psychological wellbeing and COVID-19 outcomes (vaccination, infection, long COVID) among Europeans aged ≥ 50 years old, using data from the Survey of Health, Aging, and Retirement in Europe (SHARE), specifically Wave 8 and two supplementary COVID-19 surveys (Corona Survey 1 and 2) covering 27 European countries and Israel.
Evidence base and method
The study included a total of 29,597 participants. Psychological wellbeing was measured using the Control, Autonomy, Self-realization, and Pleasure (CASP)-12 scale. Multivariable logistic regression models were performed to explore associations, with odds ratios (ORs) and 95% confidence intervals (CIs) estimated.
Consensus and conclusions
Higher psychological wellbeing was associated with increased odds of receiving a COVID-19 vaccination (OR 2.68, 95% CI: 2.44–2.96) and a significant 23% reduction in the likelihood of long COVID (OR 0.77, 95% CI: 0.64–0.93). However, no significant association was found between psychological wellbeing and the risk of SARS-CoV-2 infection (OR 0.87, 95% CI: 0.74–1.02). Protective effects were more pronounced with higher CASP-12 scores.
Controversies and gaps
The study failed to observe a significant association between psychological wellbeing and the risk of SARS-CoV-2 infection. The authors emphasized the need for further high-quality studies to confirm the findings.
Research and practice implications
Further high-quality studies are warranted to confirm the findings and explore the mechanisms underlying the associations between psychological wellbeing and COVID-19 outcomes.

深度测序严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2数据中人为假象及误导性宿主内变异株的识别与屏蔽

Identification and Masking of Artefactual and Misleading Within-Host Variants in Deep-Sequencing SARS-CoV-2 Data.
作者： Klara M. Anker, R. Evans Pena, Steven A Kemp, J. Clarke, Lele Zhao, D. Bonsall, Nicholas E Grayson, M. Bashton, A. S. Walker, T. Golubchik
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本研究聚焦于 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2,具体探讨深度测序数据中组内-宿主 变异体的人工与误导性识别及屏蔽。核心问题是如何可靠检测低频变异体,以确保进化与 传播 推断的准确性。
证据基础与方法
The review is based on deep-sequencing data from the UK's Office for National Statistics COVID-19 Infection Survey. The study uses a systematic, dataset-aware framework to identify and mask artefactual iSNVs, emphasizing recurrence within datasets to distinguish true variants from noise.
共识与结论
研究得出结论:在 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 测序数据中,反复出现的组内人工 iSNV 普遍存在,且即使在保守的 MAF 阈值下仍可能持续存在。应用数据感知框架可显著减少低频变异体的虚假共享,并从质上改变组内多样性与 传播 瓶颈规模的下游推断。
争议与缺口
研究表明,组内人工 iSNV 主要与测序中心相关而非引物特异性,可能在不同测序平台间引入结果变异性。此外,由于研究主要聚焦 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2,其他病毒受此类人工影响的程度仍有待探索。
科研与实践启示
研究结果强调,在 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 及其他快速进化病原体的基因组研究中,必须采用显式的、数据感知的人工控制措施。随着研究日益依赖亚共识多样性进行进化与流行病学洞察,此类控制尤为重要。

Identification and Masking of Artefactual and Misleading Within-Host Variants in Deep-Sequencing SARS-CoV-2 Data.

Authors: Klara M. Anker, R. Evans Pena, Steven A Kemp, J. Clarke, Lele Zhao, D. Bonsall, Nicholas E Grayson, M. Bashton, A. S. Walker, T. Golubchik
Original abstract (English)
Deep sequencing data are increasingly used to study within-host viral diversity and to inform evolutionary inference. For SARS-CoV-2, analyses based on intra-host single-nucleotide variants (iSNVs) have been widely applied to quantify within-host diversity and infer transmission dynamics. However, these applications critically depend on the reliable identification of low-frequency variants, which remain vulnerable to systematic and technical artefacts. In this study, we show that recurrent artefactual iSNVs are common in large-scale SARS-CoV-2 sequencing data and can persist even under conservative minor allele frequency (MAF) thresholds. Using data from the UK's Office for National Statistics COVID-19 Infection Survey, we demonstrate that such artefacts are predominantly sequencing centre- rather than primer-specific. Each centre exhibits a modest, distinct set of recurrent artefactual variants showing little overlap with sites routinely masked at the consensus level. To address this, we developed a systematic, dataset-aware framework that uses recurrence within sequencing datasets to identify small, noise-adapted sets of artefactual iSNVs to mask. Applying this framework reduces spurious sharing of low-frequency variants between samples and qualitatively alters downstream inferences, including estimates of within-host diversity and transmission bottleneck sizes. Although this study focussed on SARS-CoV-2, it is likely that recurrent artefactual iSNVs will be problematic for other viruses as mass-sequencing becomes increasingly routine. Together, these findings highlight the importance of explicit, dataset-aware artefact control for robust inference from within-host variation, particularly as genomic studies increasingly seek to exploit sub-consensus diversity in rapidly evolving pathogens.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
This study focuses on SARS-CoV-2, specifically examining the identification and masking of artefactual and misleading within-host variants in deep-sequencing data. The central question is how to reliably detect low-frequency variants to ensure accurate evolutionary and transmission inferences.
Evidence base and method
The review is based on deep-sequencing data from the UK's Office for National Statistics COVID-19 Infection Survey. The study uses a systematic, dataset-aware framework to identify and mask artefactual iSNVs, emphasizing recurrence within datasets to distinguish true variants from noise.
Consensus and conclusions
The study concludes that recurrent artefactual iSNVs are common in SARS-CoV-2 sequencing data and can persist even under conservative MAF thresholds. Applying a dataset-aware framework significantly reduces spurious sharing of low-frequency variants and alters downstream inferences about within-host diversity and transmission bottlenecks.
Controversies and gaps
The study highlights that artefactual iSNVs are predominantly sequencing centre-specific rather than primer-specific, which may introduce variability in results across different sequencing platforms. There is a gap in understanding how these artefacts affect other viruses, as the study primarily focuses on SARS-CoV-2.
Research and practice implications
The findings emphasize the need for explicit, dataset-aware artefact control in genomic studies of SARS-CoV-2 and other rapidly evolving pathogens. This is particularly important as research increasingly explores sub-consensus diversity for evolutionary and epidemiological insights.

家族性急性坏死性脑病与严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染的时间关联

Familial acute necrotizing encephalopathy temporally associated with SARS-CoV-2 infection
作者： Ning Zhang, Yipan Fan, Weixing Ge, Zhao Lin, Lin Zhao
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
本综述聚焦于与严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染时间关联的家族性急性坏死性脑病(ANE)。核心问题是严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染是否可导致ANE,特别是在家族背景下,以及除RANBP2基因外的遗传易感因素是否可能参与此关联。
证据基础与方法
本综述为2名在2022年12月实验室确诊感染严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2后出现ANE的同胞兄妹的病例报告。作者对这些病例进行回顾性分析,排除了其他诊断(如其他感染性脑炎、代谢性疾病、自身免疫性脑炎及中毒性脑病)。研究设计为观察性研究,未报告荟萃分析或汇总估计值。
共识与结论
两名患者的临床过程及神经影像学发现与ANE先前报道的特征一致。病例提示严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染与ANE发展之间存在潜在关联,并凸显除RANBP2基因外的遗传易感因素可能参与疾病发生。
争议与缺口
The review notes that familial COVID-19–associated ANE remains rarely reported. There is no genetic analysis confirming the involvement of RANBP2 mutations, and the study does not provide a comprehensive analysis of potential genetic factors. The findings are limited to 2 cases, which may not be sufficient to draw broad conclusions.
科研与实践启示
Further genetic and mechanistic studies are needed to identify additional susceptibility genes involved in COVID-19–associated ANE. Early recognition of neurological deterioration, prompt brain MRI evaluation, and timely initiation of immunomodulatory therapy may improve clinical outcomes.

Familial acute necrotizing encephalopathy temporally associated with SARS-CoV-2 infection

Authors: Ning Zhang, Yipan Fan, Weixing Ge, Zhao Lin, Lin Zhao
Original abstract (English)
Rationale: Acute necrotizing encephalopathy (ANE) is a rare but life-threatening parainfectious encephalopathy characterized by rapid neurological deterioration following viral infections. Although most cases occur sporadically, familial clustering is uncommon and suggests the involvement of genetic susceptibility factors. The emergence of coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) has broadened the spectrum of viral triggers associated with ANE; however, familial COVID-19–associated ANE remains rarely reported. Patient concerns: We retrospectively reviewed 2 siblings who developed ANE following laboratory-confirmed severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) infection in December 2022. The first patient was an 18-year-old woman who presented with seizures and progressive impairment of consciousness 2 days after COVID-19 infection. Her 24-year-old brother developed a similar neurological syndrome after SARS-CoV-2 infection, characterized by altered mental status and rapid clinical deterioration. Diagnose: Brain magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the female patient demonstrated typical bilateral symmetrical lesions involving the thalami, basal ganglia, corpus callosum, cerebellum, and cerebral white matter. MRI of her brother revealed bilateral symmetrical lesions predominantly affecting the lenticular and caudate nuclei. Genetic analysis showed no pathogenic variants in the RANBP2 gene. After comprehensive exclusion of alternative diagnoses, including other infectious encephalitis, metabolic disorders, autoimmune encephalitis, and toxic encephalopathy, both patients were diagnosed with COVID-19–associated ANE. Interventions: Both patients received immunomodulatory therapy, including high-dose corticosteroids and intravenous immunoglobulin. Supportive treatments and intensive neurological monitoring were also provided according to clinical conditions. Outcomes: Following treatment, both patients achieved partial neurological recovery; however, residual neurological impairment remained during short-term follow-up. The clinical course and neuroimaging findings were consistent with previously reported characteristics of ANE. Lessons: These familial cases highlight a potential association between SARS-CoV-2 infection and the development of ANE and suggest that genetic susceptibility factors other than RANBP2 mutations may contribute to disease pathogenesis. Early recognition of neurological deterioration, prompt brain MRI evaluation, and timely initiation of immunomodulatory therapy may improve clinical outcomes. Further genetic and mechanistic studies are warranted to identify additional susceptibility genes involved in COVID-19–associated ANE.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on familial acute necrotizing encephalopathy (ANE) temporally associated with SARS-CoV-2 infection. The central question is whether SARS-CoV-2 infection can lead to ANE, particularly in familial contexts, and whether genetic susceptibility factors other than RANBP2 mutations may contribute to this association.
Evidence base and method
The review is a case report of 2 siblings who developed ANE following laboratory-confirmed SARS-CoV-2 infection in December 2022. The authors conducted a retrospective analysis of these cases, excluding alternative diagnoses such as other infectious encephalitis, metabolic disorders, autoimmune encephalitis, and toxic encephalopathy. The study design is observational, with no meta-analysis or pooled estimates reported.
Consensus and conclusions
The clinical course and neuroimaging findings of the 2 patients were consistent with previously reported characteristics of ANE. The cases suggest a potential association between SARS-CoV-2 infection and the development of ANE, and highlight the possibility of genetic susceptibility factors other than RANBP2 mutations contributing to the disease pathogenesis.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes that familial COVID-19–associated ANE remains rarely reported. There is no genetic analysis confirming the involvement of RANBP2 mutations, and the study does not provide a comprehensive analysis of potential genetic factors. The findings are limited to 2 cases, which may not be sufficient to draw broad conclusions.
Research and practice implications
Further genetic and mechanistic studies are needed to identify additional susceptibility genes involved in COVID-19–associated ANE. Early recognition of neurological deterioration, prompt brain MRI evaluation, and timely initiation of immunomodulatory therapy may improve clinical outcomes.

严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 与其他呼吸道病毒感染后急性症状及相关情况风险:一项系统性综述与荟萃分析

Risk of Post-acute Symptoms and Conditions After SARS-CoV-2 Compared to Other Respiratory Viral Infections: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.
作者： Pinto TF, Andrea Santoro, Ana Laura Grossi de Oliveira, Thais Silva Tavares, André Almeida, F. Incardona, Giulia Marchetti, A. Cozzi-Lepri, J. Pinto, Caporali JFM
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述旨在比较感染严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2后出现延迟急性症状及相关疾病的风险与其他呼吸道病毒感染后的风险,重点关注这些症状是反映了严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2感染的特异性后果,还是更广泛的病毒感染后机制。
证据基础与方法
该综述是一项对队列研究研究的系统性分析,比较了成人在感染严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2后的持续症状或疾病状况与其他急性呼吸道病毒感染后的情况。研究检索了PubMed、Embase和Scopus数据库,并采用随机效应模型估计汇总风险。
共识与结论
该综述发现 严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 感染与特定急性后症状及疾病风险增加相关,包括肺栓塞、异常呼吸、疲劳、出血性脑卒中、记忆丧失/脑雾及心悸。心率异常显示边际显著性。对于大多数其他结局,汇总估计值并未较其他急性呼吸道病毒感染显著更高。
争议与缺口
综述指出,某些结局(如心率异常)的证据显示边缘性显著。此外,研究强调需进一步研究以区分共性病毒感染后机制与病原体特异性效应。
科研与实践启示
研究结果表明,识别非新型冠状病毒呼吸道病毒感染后急性期症状的重要性,并强调需进一步探索共性与病原体特异性机制。这可能为未来研究、疫苗接种策略及临床管理提供参考。

Risk of Post-acute Symptoms and Conditions After SARS-CoV-2 Compared to Other Respiratory Viral Infections: A Systematic Review and Meta-Analysis.

Authors: Pinto TF, Andrea Santoro, Ana Laura Grossi de Oliveira, Thais Silva Tavares, André Almeida, F. Incardona, Giulia Marchetti, A. Cozzi-Lepri, J. Pinto, Caporali JFM
Original abstract (English)
Background How post-COVID-19 condition (PCC) differs from post-acute infection syndromes (PAIS) caused by other respiratory viruses remains uncertain. Comparing these conditions may clarify whether post-acute symptoms reflect specific consequences of SARS-CoV-2 infection or broader post-viral mechanisms. Methods We conducted a systematic review and meta-analysis of cohort studies comparing persistent symptoms or conditions in adults after SARS-CoV-2 infection with those following other acute respiratory viral infections. PubMed, Embase, and Scopus were searched. Random-effects models were used to estimate pooled risks. Results Among 9,371 records screened, 22 studies were included and 14 contributed to the meta-analysis. Increased risk after SARS-CoV-2 infection was observed for pulmonary embolism, abnormal breathing, fatigue, hemorrhagic stroke, memory loss/brain fog, and palpitations; heart rate abnormalities showed borderline significance. For most other outcomes pooled estimates were not significantly more frequent than in other acute respiratory viral infections. Conclusions Our findings support the concept of PCC as one example within the broader spectrum of PAIS, underscoring the need to recognize these conditions after non-COVID respiratory viral infections and to further investigate shared and pathogen-specific mechanisms.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review aims to compare the risk of post-acute symptoms and conditions after SARS-CoV-2 infection with those following other respiratory viral infections, focusing on whether these symptoms reflect specific consequences of SARS-CoV-2 or broader post-viral mechanisms.
Evidence base and method
The review is a systematic analysis of cohort studies comparing persistent symptoms or conditions in adults after SARS-CoV-2 infection with those following other acute respiratory viral infections. PubMed, Embase, and Scopus were searched, and random-effects models were used to estimate pooled risks.
Consensus and conclusions
The review found that SARS-CoV-2 infection is associated with an increased risk of specific post-acute symptoms and conditions, including pulmonary embolism, abnormal breathing, fatigue, hemorrhagic stroke, memory loss/brain fog, and palpitations. Heart rate abnormalities showed borderline significance. For most other outcomes, pooled estimates were not significantly more frequent than in other acute respiratory viral infections.
Controversies and gaps
The review acknowledges that the evidence for some outcomes, such as heart rate abnormalities, was borderline significant. Additionally, the study highlights the need for further research to distinguish between shared post-viral mechanisms and pathogen-specific effects.
Research and practice implications
The findings suggest the importance of recognizing post-acute symptoms after non-COVID respiratory viral infections and emphasize the need for further investigation into both shared and pathogen-specific mechanisms. This could inform future research, surveillance, and clinical management strategies.

Post-Acute Sequelae of Non-COVID-19 Respiratory Viral Infections: A Systematic Review.

Post-Acute Sequelae of Non-COVID-19 Respiratory Viral Infections: A Systematic Review.
作者： Esther Hui‐En Chong, Wee IJY, Jenny G. Low, Paul Anantharajah Tambyah, Dorothy Hui Lin Ng
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
Respiratory viral infections (RVIs) affect millions annually, yet long-term consequences outside COVID-19 are poorly defined.
证据基础与方法
主要数据库自2004年1月至2025年8月检索观察性研究,纳入症状、功能损害或新诊断出现于RVIs后≥4周的研究。使用ROBINS-I评估偏倚风险,并采用GRADE方法确定证据确定性。
共识与结论
流感是研究最多的病原体,并持续与长期肺功能障碍、弥散功能降低、呼吸困难、疲劳及生活质量下降相关。大型队列研究证实了显著的肺部发病率及较少见的心血管和神经精神负担。
争议与缺口
整体而言,现有证据提示非新型冠状病毒感染RVIs与急性期后多器官后遗症相关,尤其是重症感染后。然而,由于研究设计不一致、病原体覆盖有限及证据确定性低,证据仍支离破碎。
科研与实践启示
亟需标准化、前瞻性、病原体特异性研究以完善这些初步信号,并指导临床随访及预防策略。

Post-Acute Sequelae of Non-COVID-19 Respiratory Viral Infections: A Systematic Review.

Authors: Esther Hui‐En Chong, Wee IJY, Jenny G. Low, Paul Anantharajah Tambyah, Dorothy Hui Lin Ng
Original abstract (English)
Respiratory viral infections (RVIs) affect millions annually, yet long-term consequences outside COVID-19 are poorly defined. Post-acute sequelae, encompassing persistent or new physical, cognitive, and mental health impairments, are increasingly recognised as clinically important. We systematically reviewed evidence on post-acute outcomes in adults following non-COVID RVIs to characterise pathogen-specific patterns and highlight areas for future research. Major databases were searched from January 2004 to August 2025 for observational studies reporting symptoms, functional impairment, or new diagnoses ≥ 4 weeks after RVIs. Study characteristics, pathogens, follow-up duration, and organ-specific outcomes were extracted. Risk of bias was evaluated using ROBINS-I, and certainty of evidence was determined with the GRADE approach. Narrative synthesis was performed owing to heterogeneity. Thirteen studies (n = 631,863) met eligibility criteria. Influenza was the most studied pathogen and consistently associated with long-term pulmonary dysfunction, reduced diffusion capacity, dyspnoea, fatigue, and poorer quality of life. Large cohorts corroborated substantial pulmonary morbidity and less frequent cardiovascular and neuropsychiatric burdens. Two RSV cohorts (n = 1553 and n = 471) showed a predominance of cardiovascular sequelae; respiratory and neurological outcomes were less frequent. A single HMPV cohort (n = 92) reported persistent symptoms in 55% of adults at 90-270 days, most commonly dyspnoea, fatigue, and cough. Overall, the available evidence suggests that non-COVID RVIs are associated with post-acute multi-organ sequelae, particularly following severe infection. Nevertheless, evidence remains fragmented due to inconsistent study designs, limited pathogen coverage, and low certainty. Standardised, prospective, pathogen-specific studies are required to refine these preliminary signals and inform clinical follow-up and prevention strategies.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Respiratory viral infections (RVIs) affect millions annually, yet long-term consequences outside COVID-19 are poorly defined.
Evidence base and method
Major databases were searched from January 2004 to August 2025 for observational studies reporting symptoms, functional impairment, or new diagnoses ≥ 4 weeks after RVIs. Risk of bias was evaluated using ROBINS-I, and certainty of evidence was determined with the GRADE approach.
Consensus and conclusions
Influenza was the most studied pathogen and consistently associated with long-term pulmonary dysfunction, reduced diffusion capacity, dyspnoea, fatigue, and poorer quality of life. Large cohorts corroborated substantial pulmonary morbidity and less frequent cardiovascular and neuropsychiatric burdens.
Controversies and gaps
Overall, the available evidence suggests that non-COVID RVIs are associated with post-acute multi-organ sequelae, particularly following severe infection. Nevertheless, evidence remains fragmented due to inconsistent study designs, limited pathogen coverage, and low certainty.
Research and practice implications
Standardised, prospective, pathogen-specific studies are required to refine these preliminary signals and inform clinical follow-up and prevention strategies.

基于计算机模拟方法在严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2病毒入侵与复制抑制剂设计中的最新进展

Recent Advances in the Design of Inhibitors Targeting the Viral Entry and Replication of the SARS-CoV-2 Virus, Driven by In Silico Approaches
作者： Phumelele Nodola, Patience S. S. Molefe, Potlaki F. Tseki, Njabulo J. Gumede
摘要中文翻译严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2疫情对全球健康、政治、医学、金融及社会产生了重大影响。自2020年以来,已报告多种突变,导致现有治疗对不同严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2株产生耐药性,且长新冠病例激增。这一局势凸显了开发针对性强且有效药物以遏制严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2株及其突变株传播、管理长新冠症状并应对未来大流行的紧迫需求。目前,严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2的治疗策略主要聚焦于靶向病毒入侵与复制机制,以干扰其生命周期。本综述检视了已获批药物、临床候选药物及研发中的抑制剂,并结合其生物测定数据,同时强调相关挑战。文中阐述了抑制剂与活性位点结合的机制,为研究者提供见解,并强调计算机模拟研究(如分子对接、分子动力学模拟、FEP+、WaterMap及定量构效关系(QSAR)分析)与实验研究的相关性,从而加速药物发现进程。本综述旨在为研究者提供关于亟待解决的突变影响病毒入侵的差距见解,并为未来大流行做好准备。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
The review targets SARS-CoV-2, COVID-19, and named SARS-CoV-2 variants (e.g., Omicron, JN.1, BA.2.86), focusing on the central question of designing inhibitors that disrupt viral entry and replication mechanisms to combat current strains and prepare for future pandemics.
证据基础与方法
本综述为叙事综合分析,检视了已获批药物、临床候选药物及研发中的抑制剂,并结合其生物测定数据与计算机模拟方法(如分子对接、分子动力学模拟、FEP+、WaterMap及QSAR分析),如综述范围与方法学重点所述。
共识与结论
综述强调,靶向病毒入侵与复制机制是严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 治疗的主要策略,而计算机模拟研究在通过计算预测与实验验证相关性方面发挥关键作用,从而加速药物发现。
争议与缺口
综述指出了未解决的挑战,包括严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2 突变对耐药性的影响及长新冠症状管理,这些问题使治疗效力复杂化,并凸显了在应对变异株驱动的耐药性及急性后遗症方面的研究差距。
科研与实践启示
综述优先开发针对严重急性呼吸综合征冠状病毒2变异株耐药性的靶向抑制剂,并呼吁通过利用计算机模拟工具加速药物发现及改善治疗策略,以更好地应对未来大流行。

Recent Advances in the Design of Inhibitors Targeting the Viral Entry and Replication of the SARS-CoV-2 Virus, Driven by In Silico Approaches

Authors: Phumelele Nodola, Patience S. S. Molefe, Potlaki F. Tseki, Njabulo J. Gumede
Original abstract (English)
The SARS-CoV-2 pandemic has significantly impacted global health, politics, medicine, finance, and society. Since 2020, various mutations have been reported, leading to drug resistance in current treatments against different SARS-CoV-2 strains and a drastic increase in cases of long-COVID. This situation underscores the urgent need to develop targeted and effective drugs to combat the spread of SARS-CoV-2 strains and their mutants, manage long-COVID symptoms and prepare for future pandemics. Currently, the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 focuses on targeting the virus’s entry and replication mechanisms to disrupt its life cycle. This review examines approved drugs, clinical candidates, and inhibitors under development, along with their bioassay data, while highlighting associated challenges. It illustrates how inhibitors bind to active sites, providing insights and emphasizing the importance of in silico studies, such as molecular docking, molecular dynamics simulation, FEP+, WaterMap, and quantitative structure–activity relationship (QSAR) analyses, and their correlation with experimental studies in expediting the drug discovery process. The review aims to provide researchers with insights into the gaps that need to be addressed concerning mutations affecting viral entry and to prepare for future pandemics.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review targets SARS-CoV-2, COVID-19, and named SARS-CoV-2 variants (e.g., Omicron, JN.1, BA.2.86), focusing on the central question of designing inhibitors that disrupt viral entry and replication mechanisms to combat current strains and prepare for future pandemics.
Evidence base and method
The review is a narrative synthesis examining approved drugs, clinical candidates, and inhibitors under development, alongside their bioassay data and in silico methodologies such as molecular docking, molecular dynamics simulation, FEP+, WaterMap, and QSAR analyses, as illustrated by the review's scope and methodological emphasis.
Consensus and conclusions
The review highlights that targeting viral entry and replication mechanisms is the primary strategy for SARS-CoV-2 treatment, with in silico studies playing a critical role in expediting drug discovery by correlating computational predictions with experimental validation.
Controversies and gaps
The review identifies unresolved challenges, including the impact of SARS-CoV-2 mutations on drug resistance and the management of long-COVID symptoms, which complicate treatment efficacy and underscore gaps in addressing variant-driven resistance and post-acute sequelae.
Research and practice implications
The review prioritizes the development of targeted inhibitors to address drug resistance in SARS-CoV-2 variants and calls for enhanced preparation for future pandemics by leveraging in silico tools to accelerate drug discovery and improve treatment strategies.

The CDC’s management and COVID-19 vaccine recommendations during the COVID-19 pandemic: A healthcare worker’s perspective

The CDC’s management and COVID-19 vaccine recommendations during the COVID-19 pandemic: A healthcare worker’s perspective
作者： Gail C. D’Souza-Rushton, Uttara Seshu, Liba Blumberger, Laraib Mazhar, Jessica M. Yingst, Jonathan Foulds, William A. Calo, Casey N. Pinto, D'Souza-Rushton GC, Gail Carmen D’Souza
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
The CDC’s management of the COVID-19 pandemic and vaccine recommendations were the focus of the study, with the aim of understanding healthcare workers’ (HCWs) perceptions and acceptance of these guidelines to improve patient outcomes.
证据基础与方法
该研究基于对宾夕法尼亚州医护工作者(HCWs)的一项在线调查,调查时间为2022年8月至2023年2月。数据收集方法包括收集HCWs的疫苗接种状态及首次疫苗接种时间的相关信息。审查方法为描述性研究,侧重于对HCWs观点的定性分析。
共识与结论
大多数医护工作者(85%)至少接种过一剂疫苗疫苗,且普遍认为CDC的疫情管理清晰、科学驱动且有效。然而,批评意见包括对口罩要求的过度管控、政治化倾向及信息传递的不一致。
争议与缺口
对CDC管理的看法褒贬不一,批评包括过度管控、政治化倾向及政策变动。此外,对疫苗疫苗强制令及信息传递不一致的担忧可能影响医护工作者对指南的接受度与遵循度。
科研与实践启示
CDC应确保未来信息传递避免感知上的政治偏见,并保持与医护工作者的开放沟通,以在疫情期间提供基于证据的信息。提升政策沟通的清晰度与一致性,有助于增强医护工作者对指南的信任与遵循度。

The CDC’s management and COVID-19 vaccine recommendations during the COVID-19 pandemic: A healthcare worker’s perspective

Authors: Gail C. D’Souza-Rushton, Uttara Seshu, Liba Blumberger, Laraib Mazhar, Jessica M. Yingst, Jonathan Foulds, William A. Calo, Casey N. Pinto, D'Souza-Rushton GC, Gail Carmen D’Souza
Original abstract (English)
The CDC’s COVID-19 pandemic guidelines affected everyone, especially healthcare workers (HCWs) due to the high exposure risks, long hours and stress during the pandemic. Thus, understanding HCWs perceptions and acceptance of these guidelines were crucial to patient outcomes. Our goal was to investigate HCWs views on the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) management of the COVID-19 pandemic and vaccine recommendations. Data were collected from an online survey of HCWs in Pennsylvania (August 2022 – February 2023). Eligible participants (n = 580) aged 21 + , reported COVID-19 vaccination status and time of first vaccination. Participants (n = 580) were 83% female and 92% White. From our sample, 71% provided direct care, 57% lived in a suburban area, and 85% had received at least one dose of the vaccine. HCWs had mixed perceptions of the CDC’s management of the COVID-19 pandemic and vaccine recommendations. Positive perceptions of pandemic management highlighted clear communication, a science-driven approach, and an effective crisis response. Criticisms included overregulation of face mask requirements, politicization, and continuously changing policies. Regarding vaccine recommendations, positive perceptions included timely distribution, accessibility, affordability, and clear communication, while criticisms included vaccine mandates, politicization, perceived lack of scientific basis, and inconsistent messaging. Future recommendations for the CDC regarding outbreaks would be to ensure framing of messaging is free from perceived political bias. The CDC should continue to maintain communication with HCWs and focus on providing evidence-based information during future pandemics.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The CDC’s management of the COVID-19 pandemic and vaccine recommendations were the focus of the study, with the aim of understanding healthcare workers’ (HCWs) perceptions and acceptance of these guidelines to improve patient outcomes.
Evidence base and method
The study was based on an online survey of HCWs in Pennsylvania conducted between August 2022 and February 2023. The data collection method involved gathering information on HCWs’ vaccination status and timing of first vaccination. The review method was descriptive, focusing on qualitative analysis of HCWs’ perspectives.
Consensus and conclusions
The majority of HCWs (85%) had received at least one dose of the vaccine, and there was a general perception of the CDC’s pandemic management as clear, science-driven, and effective. However, there were criticisms regarding overregulation of face mask requirements, politicization, and inconsistent messaging.
Controversies and gaps
There were mixed perceptions of the CDC’s management, with criticisms about overregulation, politicization, and changing policies. Additionally, there were concerns about vaccine mandates and inconsistent messaging, which may have affected HCWs’ acceptance and adherence to guidelines.
Research and practice implications
The CDC should ensure that future messaging is free from perceived political bias and maintain open communication with HCWs to provide evidence-based information during pandemics. Improving clarity and consistency in policy communication could enhance HCWs’ trust and adherence to guidelines.

Disparities in COVID-19 Vaccination and Implications for Hospital-Based Interventions.

Disparities in COVID-19 Vaccination and Implications for Hospital-Based Interventions.
作者： Christina R. Bergin, Cassandra J. Everly, Ruth Franks Snedecor, Denege Ward-Wright, Pooja Rangan, NAFIS SHAMSID-DEEN
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
The review examines disparities in COVID-19 vaccination rates and explores how hospital-based interventions can address these disparities, particularly in populations with limited access to care. The focus is on SARS-CoV-2 and its variants, with an emphasis on structural inequities and social determinants influencing vaccination outcomes.
证据基础与方法
本综述基于对超过 33,700 名成年患者的回顾性分析,分析了 疫苗接种 发病率并识别出未接种疫苗的独立风险因素,包括社会健康决定因素。该分析将结果与全球公共卫生和医学文献整合,以强调医疗系统在改善疫苗接种获取和接种率方面的影响。
共识与结论
本综述得出结论:医院可在降低面临健康差异的患者 疫苗接种 障碍方面发挥关键作用。强调需解决结构性不平等和社会决定因素,以改善疫苗接种获取和接种率。
争议与缺口
The review notes that while hospitals offer pneumococcal and influenza vaccines, they have not offered COVID-19 vaccines in some cases. This decision may reflect a missed opportunity to address disparities in vaccination. The evidence also highlights the need for more research on the impact of health system interventions on vaccine access.
科研与实践启示
The review suggests that hospitals should consider implementing targeted interventions to improve access to and uptake of COVID-19 vaccines, especially for vulnerable populations. It also calls for further research on the effectiveness of such interventions and the role of health systems in addressing vaccine disparities.

Disparities in COVID-19 Vaccination and Implications for Hospital-Based Interventions.

Authors: Christina R. Bergin, Cassandra J. Everly, Ruth Franks Snedecor, Denege Ward-Wright, Pooja Rangan, NAFIS SHAMSID-DEEN
Original abstract (English)
Structural inequities and social determinants influence vaccination rates and related health outcomes. In 2025 in the USA, reduced federal funding for state and local vaccination programs and revised FDA COVID-19 vaccine policy have created new logistical and financial barriers to immunization, particularly for patients already facing limited access to care. This perspective highlights the role hospitals can play in reducing these barriers, through an example case study of a large academic medical center in Phoenix, AZ. Although this hospital routinely offers pneumococcal and annual influenza vaccines to inpatients, it has elected not to offer COVID-19 vaccines. To evaluate this potential missed opportunity, we conducted a retrospective review of more than 33,700 adult patients, analyzing COVID-19 vaccination rates and identifying independent risk factors for being unvaccinated, including measures of social determinants of health. We integrate findings from this retrospective analysis with global public health and medical literature to highlight the significant impact that health systems can have on improving vaccine access and uptake for patients experiencing health disparities.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines disparities in COVID-19 vaccination rates and explores how hospital-based interventions can address these disparities, particularly in populations with limited access to care. The focus is on SARS-CoV-2 and its variants, with an emphasis on structural inequities and social determinants influencing vaccination outcomes.
Evidence base and method
The review is based on a retrospective analysis of more than 33,700 adult patients, examining vaccination rates and identifying independent risk factors for being unvaccinated, including social determinants of health. It integrates findings with global public health and medical literature to highlight the impact of health systems on vaccine access and uptake.
Consensus and conclusions
The review concludes that hospitals can play a critical role in reducing barriers to vaccination for patients experiencing health disparities. It emphasizes the importance of addressing structural inequities and social determinants to improve vaccine access and uptake.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes that while hospitals offer pneumococcal and influenza vaccines, they have not offered COVID-19 vaccines in some cases. This decision may reflect a missed opportunity to address disparities in vaccination. The evidence also highlights the need for more research on the impact of health system interventions on vaccine access.
Research and practice implications
The review suggests that hospitals should consider implementing targeted interventions to improve access to and uptake of COVID-19 vaccines, especially for vulnerable populations. It also calls for further research on the effectiveness of such interventions and the role of health systems in addressing vaccine disparities.

The report does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, nor does it mention any health-system effects or operational impacts. The source is a government website, and the content is a full original report. The event is a political statement and not a confirmed health-related incident.

COVID-19 dashboard

COVID-19 dashboard
新闻简报The WHO COVID-19 dashboard provides a global summary of confirmed SARS-CoV-2 cases and deaths, with data collected from countries, territories, and areas. It includes information on case definitions, testing strategies, and reporting practices, which vary by region. The dashboard has been updated since March 2020, with weekly data from July 2023 onwards. Data is subject to continuous verification and may change due to retrospective updates or shifts in reporting standards. WHO acknowledges that case detection, testing, and reporting practices differ globally, which can lead to under or overestimation of true case and death counts. Additionally, some countries have stopped reporting or changed their reporting frequency, which may affect the completeness of the data. The dashboard serves as a tool for monitoring the pandemic's spread and impact, but it is important to note that the data reflects official reports and may not capture all cases or deaths accurately.
查看新闻五要素
时间
从2019年12月31日至2020年3月21日,以及自2020年3月22日起,从2023年7月起每周更新(N14、N15、N21)。
地点与对象
全球,数据收集自国家、地区和地区,包括高收入、低收入、中低收入和中上收入群体(N1、N16)。
事件
WHO monitors and reports on the global epidemiological situation of SARS-CoV-2 through the WHO COVID-19 dashboard, including confirmed cases and deaths (N1, N4, N10).
规模、影响与风险
该仪表板包括向世卫组织报告的确诊病例和死亡病例,数据需要经过验证,并且由于回顾性更新或报告做法的变化而可能发生变化(N1、N8、N18)。
应对、状态与不确定性
世卫组织收集和传播其会员国报告的数据,可能存在重大数据错误和更正,并承认各地区在病例发现、检测策略和报告做法方面存在差异(N13、N9、N18)。
A high priorityPublic-health news

COVID-19 dashboard

News Brief
The WHO COVID-19 dashboard provides a global summary of confirmed SARS-CoV-2 cases and deaths, with data collected from countries, territories, and areas. It includes information on case definitions, testing strategies, and reporting practices, which vary by region. The dashboard has been updated since March 2020, with weekly data from July 2023 onwards. Data is subject to continuous verification and may change due to retrospective updates or shifts in reporting standards. WHO acknowledges that case detection, testing, and reporting practices differ globally, which can lead to under or overestimation of true case and death counts. Additionally, some countries have stopped reporting or changed their reporting frequency, which may affect the completeness of the data. The dashboard serves as a tool for monitoring the pandemic's spread and impact, but it is important to note that the data reflects official reports and may not capture all cases or deaths accurately.

epidemiological situation via the WHO COVID-19 dashboard. If data for certain countries is unavailable in this section, it may indicate that they have either ceased reporting COVID-19 surveillance data to WHO or have integrated the COVID-19 surveillance into existing respiratory disease surveillance. In the latter case, SARS-CoV-2 detections from sentinel and systematic virological surveillance sites in those countries may be found in the Circulation section which also includes information on SARS-CoV-2 variant circulation. This global summary of COVID-19 cases includes data on confirmed cases reported to WHO from the comprehensive COVID-19 case monitoring. Coronavirus (COVID-19) cases, %%COUNTRY%% data Source: World Health Organization Loading... Loading... Loading... What is the definition of 'confirmed' and 'reported' in COVID-19 data? All data represents the date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. In the context of WHO's statistical reporting of COVID-19 data, it is important to note that only confirmed cases are included in case and death counts. In guidance updated on 22 July 2023; there are two alternative definitions for 'Confirmed case of SARS-CoV-2 infection' in international surveillance reporting, although some departures may exist due to local adaptations: a) A person with a positive Nucleic Acid Amplification Test (NAAT), regardless of clinical criteria OR epidemiological criteria. b) A person meeting clinical criteria AND/OR epidemiological criteria (suspect case A) with a positive professional-use or self-test SARS-CoV-2 Antigen-RDT. Additionally, WHO only disseminates data as reported by its Member States. From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data has been compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported directly to WHO headquarters by Member States. Statistical counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these statistics at a global level. Please note that statistics do not necessarily reflect the actual number of cases and deaths or the actual number of countries where cases and deaths are occurring, as a number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting. Trends in COVID-19 cases, %%COUNTRY%% Recent COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, July 2023 - present Source: World Health Organization Data may be incomplete for the latest week. Total COVID-19 cases reported to WHO (weekly) %%COUNTRY%%, January 2020 - present Source: World Health Organization Why is COVID-19 data being presented as weekly statistics? A number of countries have stopped reporting or changed their frequency of reporting COVID-19 case and death counts to WHO. An outcome of these differences in reporting is that WHO may receive daily data from some countries, while other countries may only report data to WHO once every 14 days. In addition, countries differ in how they choose to report statistics; some countries provide their data attributed to specific dates while others who report less frequently may group data from 7 days into a single statistic attributed to a week in their reporting. As of 25 August 2023, WHO declared that it is no longer necessary for Member States to report daily counts of cases and deaths to WHO and requested strengthening of weekly reporting. Reported data is still available attributed to specific dates (daily data) here as a download. This dashboard presents the same statistics as weekly figures in charts in order to mitigate against the visual misinterpretation of data. Should daily data have been presented here, many countries would show zero counts for multiple consecutive days due in part to the differences in how they choose to report. While weekly intervals do not completely mitigate against this, the approach reduces the risk that some dashboard users might infer zero cases or deaths when lack of data is often due to reporting differences. What do negative counts of cases or deaths mean? All data is subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected by Member States at more frequent intervals with some countries performing retrospective bulk corrections in their reporting. Bulk corrections may lead to the appearance of significant spikes or negative values which are verified and validated by WHO. Aggregate groups (weekly data to week end date) Source: World Health Organization COVID-19 cases, country level trends Percent change based on previous 28 days Source: World Health Organization Metadata Glossary and definitions Confirmed COVID-19 case Although COVID-19 defines the symptomatic disease caused by SARS-CoV-2, the cases are presented in this dashboard meet one of two alternate definitions of confirmed case of SARS-CoV-2 infection in international surveillance reporting: a) A person with a positive Nucleic Acid Amplification Test (NAAT), regardless of clinical criteria* OR epidemiological criteria*. b) A person meeting clinical criteria* AND/OR epidemiological criteria* (suspect case A) with a positive professional-use or self-test SARS-CoV-2 Antigen-RDT. *Please visit the WHO COVID-19 Case definition for details on clinical and epidemiological criteria. Updated 22 July 2023 Last 7 days ‘Last 7 days’ refers to the most recent complete epidemiological week (commonly referred to as an 'epi week'). Last 28 days ‘Last 28 days’ refers to the four preceding most recently complete epidemiological weeks. WHO Regions The World Health Organization (WHO) is divided into six regions with regional offices, each responsible for addressing health challenges and promoting public health within its designated geographical area. These regions serve as administrative units that allow the WHO to tailor its efforts to the specific needs, priorities, and health conditions of member countries within each region. World Bank Income Groups The World Bank classifies economies into income groups based on their Gross National Income (GNI) per capita. These annually updated classifications help the World Bank and other organizations analyze and understand global economic trends, allocate resources, and design development policies. Data sources COVID-19 case data From the 31 December 2019 to the 21 March 2020, WHO collected the numbers of confirmed COVID-19 cases and deaths through official communications under the International Health Regulations (IHR, 2005), complemented by monitoring the official ministries of health websites and social media accounts. Since 22 March 2020, global data is compiled through WHO region-specific dashboards, and/or aggregate count data reported to WHO headquarters. WHO COVID-19 Dashboard is updated every Tuesday for the period of two full weeks prior. Counts primarily reflect laboratory-confirmed cases and deaths, based upon WHO case definitions; although some departures may exist due to local adaptations. Counts include both domestic and repatriated cases. Case detection, definitions, testing strategies, reporting practice, and lag times (e.g. time to case notification, and time to reporting of deaths) differ between countries, territories and areas. These factors, amongst others, influence the counts presented with variable under or overestimation of true case and death counts, and variable delays to reflecting these data at a global level. All data represent date of reporting as opposed to date of symptom onset. All data are subject to continuous verification and may change based on retrospective updates to accurately reflect trends, changes in country case definitions and/or reporting practices. Significant data errors detected or reported to WHO may be corrected at more frequent intervals. New case and death counts from the Region of the Americas Starting from the week commencing on 11 September 2023, the source of the data from the Region of the Americas was switched to the aggregated national surveillances, received through the COVID-19, Influenza, RSV and Other Respiratory Viruses program in the Americas. Data have been included retrospectively since 31 July 2023. Rates <0.001 per 100,000 population may be rounded to 0. Britannica Editors Last updated Aug. 18, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Table of Contents Table of Contents Ask Anything News • Ex-Fauci adviser pleads guilty to plotting to conceal COVID-19 research records during pandemic • Aug. 18, 2026, 8:02 PM ET (AP) ...(Show more) The Latest: Senate committee votes to hold Fauci in contempt over refusal to answer questions • Aug. 6, 2026, 7:47 PM ET (AP) Senate committee votes to hold Fauci in contempt for refusing to answer COVID questions • Aug. 6, 2026, 6:14 PM ET (AP) Fauci contempt vote reflects the enduring politics of COVID • Aug. 6, 2026, 3:14 PM ET (AP) Fauci invokes the Fifth Amendment and declines to testify in GOP-led Senate panel's COVID-19 hearing • July 29, 2026, 1:37 PM ET (AP) Show less COVID-19, highly contagious respiratory illness, the cause of the COVID-19 pandemic. COVID-19 was first detected in 2019 in Wuhan, China. A large proportion of infections in China were undocumented before travel restrictions and other control measures were implemented in late January 2020. As a result, COVID-19 very quickly spread to countries worldwide, giving rise to a multiyear pandemic that resulted in millions of deaths. The pandemic also spurred a revolution in business and education, spawning a reliance on distance learning and work-at-home arrangements and the rise of videoconferencing platforms such as Zoom, which became one of the most downloaded applications worldwide and a household word. Read more about the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. COVID-19 is caused by a coronavirus known as severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2). The disease is transmitted primarily through contact with infectious material, particularly respiratory droplets that enter the environment when an infected person sneezes or coughs. Individuals nearby may inhale or come into contact with these droplets, resulting in disease transmission. Infection may also occur when a person comes into contact with a contaminated surface and then touches his or her mouth, nose, or eyes. Individuals at greatest risk of COVID-19 infection include older adults and persons with chronic illness, largely because of weakened immune function. Britannica Quiz A Visit with the Word Doctor: Medical Vocabulary Quiz COVID-19 is characterized by a variety of symptoms, including fever, cough, congestion, fatigue, shortness of breath, headache, sore throat, nausea or vomiting, loss of smell or taste, and body aches. COVID-19 may progress to severe respiratory illness, with symptoms of chest pain and extreme difficulty in breathing, requiring hospitalization. Some COVID-19 patients who are hospitalized further develop neurological symptoms, including severe fatigue and altered consciousness. Delirium has been observed in many of these patients as well, possibly as a side effect of medication. Delirium and lingering psychological issues, including depression and anxiety, can prolong and complicate recovery. COVID-19 vaccineDoctor holding a syringe and bottle with COVID-19 vaccine.(more) There is no cure for COVID-19. However, different types of drugs have been used to treat infection and to reduce the severity of the disease. Examples include antiviral drugs, such as remdesivir, molnupiravir, and combined ritonavir and nirmatrelvir; a drug used for pancreatic inflammation called camostat mesilate; and various therapeutic monoclonal antibodies, such as REGEN-COV (casirivimab and imdevimab). COVID-19 vaccines, which became available in late 2020, are highly effective in protecting against severe illness and in limiting the spread of the disease; immunity can be further bolstered through subsequent booster doses of vaccine. Risk of disease transmission can be reduced by measures such as social distancing, disinfection of surfaces, and universal community use of face masks. Self-isolation and self-quarantine are other ways in which the spread of COVID-19 can be stopped. Survivors of severe COVID-19, particularly those who were hospitalized, are likely to suffer long-term effects. Individuals who required mechanical ventilation might never fully recover; ventilator use is associated with severe muscle atrophy and weakness, which significantly impact survival and quality of life. How We Develop New VaccinesWorried about diphtheria? No? Thank vaccines.(more) See all videos for this article The ongoing spread of COVID-19 has been fueled by the emergence of variants in evolving lineages of SARS-CoV-2. Such variants generally carry mutations that strengthen characteristics such as the virus’s ability to infect individuals (including individuals who are vaccinated and who previously had COVID-19), to cause severe disease, and to potentially escape certain treatments. There are numerous variants, the most notable of which is Omicron, which was first detected in Botswana in November 2021 and quickly became the primary circulating strain globally. Omicron spawned multiple sublineages of concern, including BA.4 and BA.5, which are highly infectious, and BA.2, which gave rise to XBB.1.5—a highly transmissible variant, noted for its heightened ability to bind to cells and to replicate. Britannica Editors Last updated July 21, 2026 •History Britannica AI Ask Anything Quick Summary Table of Contents Table of Contents Quick Summary Ask Anything Top Questions What is a pandemic? A pandemic is an outbreak of infectious disease that occurs over a wide geographical area and that is of high prevalence. A pandemic generally affects a significant proportion of the world’s population, usually over the course of several months. What have been some of the world’s deadliest pandemics? Throughout history, there have been many deadly pandemics, but the Black Death and the influenza pandemic of 1918–19 rank among the most lethal. The Black Death, which ravaged Europe between 1347 and 1351 and likely was caused by plague, killed roughly 25 million people. The influenza pandemic of 1918–19, or “Spanish flu,” claimed an estimated 20–40 million lives. A COVID-19 outbreak that was declared a pandemic in 2020 also killed millions of people. What causes a pandemic? Pandemics can be caused by several factors. For example, in some cases, a new strain or subtype of virus that first emerged in animals jumps to humans and then becomes readily transmissible between humans. In other instances, an existing disease-causing agent mutates, increasing its infectiousness. How do pandemics end? Pandemics typically slow and come to an end on their own, though the process may be accelerated through effective preventive strategies, such as improved personal hygiene or the development of a vaccine. Some pandemics, however, occur in waves, such that decreased disease activity may be followed by another period of high disease prevalence, thereby prolonging the outbreak. pandemic, outbreak of infectious disease that occurs over a wide geographical area and that is of high prevalence, generally affecting a significant proportion of the world’s population, usually over the course of several months. Pandemics arise from epidemics, which are outbreaks of disease confined to one part of the world, such as a single country. Pandemics, especially those involving influenza, sometimes occur in waves, so that a postpandemic phase, marked by decreased disease activity, may be followed by another period of high disease prevalence. Read more about the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. Infectious diseases such as influenza can spread rapidly—sometimes in a matter of days—among humans living in different areas of the world. The spread of a disease is facilitated by several factors, including an increased degree of infectiousness of the disease-causing agent, human-to-human transmission of the disease, and modern means of transportation, such as air travel. The majority of highly infectious illnesses that occur in humans are caused by diseases that first arise in animals. Thus, when a new infectious agent or disease emerges in animals, surveillance organizations located within affected areas are responsible for alerting the World Health Organization (WHO) and for closely monitoring the behaviour of the infectious agent and the activity and spread of the disease. WHO constantly monitors disease activity on a global scale through a network of surveillance centres located in countries worldwide. Epidemic vs. pandemic: What's the difference?Questions and answers about epidemics and pandemics.(more) See all videos for this article In the case of influenza, which is the disease that poses the greatest pandemic threat to humans, WHO has organized a pandemic preparedness plan that consists of six phases of pandemic alert, outlined as follows: Discover how ordinary objects work, and expand your understanding of the familiar. SIGN UP Thank you for signing up! " height="20" alt="HEW Logo"> Thank you for signing up!" Back to homepage By clicking "Sign Up", you consent to the Britannica Privacy Policy and Terms of Use. Britannica Quiz A Visit with the Word Doctor: Medical Vocabulary Quiz Phase 1: the lowest level of pandemic alert; indicates that an influenza virus, either newly emerged or previously existing, is circulating among animals. The risk of transmission to humans is low. Phase 2: isolated incidences of animal-to-human transmission of the virus are observed, indicating that the virus has pandemic potential. Phase 3: characterized by small outbreaks of disease, generally resulting from multiple cases of animal-to-human transmission, though limited capacity for human-to-human transmission may be present. Phase 4: confirmed human-to-human viral transmission that causes sustained disease in human communities. At this stage, containment of the virus is deemed impossible but a pandemic is not necessarily inevitable. The implementation of control methods to prevent further viral spread is emphasized in affected parts of the world. Phase 5: marked by human-to-human disease transmission in two countries, indicating that a pandemic is imminent and that distribution of stockpiled drugs and execution of strategies to control the disease must be carried out with a sense of urgency. Phase 6: characterized by widespread and sustained disease transmission among humans. When WHO upgrades the level of a pandemic alert, such as from level 4 to level 5, it serves as a signal to countries worldwide to implement the appropriate predetermined disease-control strategies. second pandemic of the Black Death in EuropeThe second pandemic of the Black Death in Europe (1347–51).(more) Throughout history, pandemics of diseases such as cholera, plague, and influenza have played a major role in shaping human civilizations. Examples of significant historical pandemics include the plague pandemic of the Byzantine Empire in the 6th century ce; the Black Death, which originated in China and spread across Europe in the 14th century; and the influenza pandemic of 1918–19, which originated in the U.S. state of Kansas and spread to Europe, Asia, and islands in the South Pacific. Although pandemics are typically characterized by their occurrence over a short span of time, today several infectious diseases persist at a high level of incidence, occur on a global scale, and can be transmitted between humans either directly or indirectly. Such diseases represented in modern pandemics include AIDS, caused by HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), which is transmitted directly between humans; and malaria, caused by parasites in the genus Plasmodium, which are transmitted from one human to another by mosquitoes that feed on the blood of infected humans. Influenza pandemics are estimated to occur roughly once every 50 years, though the actual pandemic interval has in some instances been shorter than this. For example, following the 1918–19 pandemic, there were two other 20th-century influenza pandemics: the 1957 Asian flu pandemic and the 1968 Hong Kong flu pandemic. The virus that caused the 1957 pandemic, which lasted until about the middle of 1958, was also responsible for a series of epidemics that emerged annually until 1968, when the Hong Kong flu appeared. The Hong Kong flu pandemic, which lasted until 1969–70, caused between one million and four million deaths. The next influenza pandemic occurred in 2009, when a subtype of H1N1 virus spread across multiple regions of the world. Between March 2009 and mid-January 2010, more than 14,140 laboratory-confirmed H1N1 deaths had been reported worldwide. The devastating history of pandemicsOverview of pandemics in human history, including the novel coronavirus pandemic of 2020.(more) See all videos for this article In March 2020 an ongoing outbreak of a novel coronavirus known as severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus-2 (SARS-CoV2) was declared a pandemic by WHO officials. Infection with SARS-CoV2 produced an illness known as coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19); the illness was characterized primarily by fever, cough, and shortness of breath. The outbreak began in late 2019 in Wuhan, China, when a patient with pneumonia of unknown cause was admitted to a local hospital. In the following weeks, the number of people infected with the novel virus grew rapidly in Wuhan, and the disease spread to other regions of China. By early 2020 COVID-19 had reached Europe and the United States, carried there by travelers coming from affected regions. By the time the outbreak was declared a pandemic, cases of COVID-19 had been detected in numerous countries worldwide, with about 130,000 confirmed cases and close to 5,000 deaths. The official World Health Organization total of more than seven million deaths as of March 2024 is widely considered a serious undercount of the actual toll. In some countries there was limited testing for the virus and difficulty attributing fatalities to it. The article discusses the origin and characteristics of SARS-CoV-2, the virus that causes COVID-19, including its transmission methods, symptoms, and impact on global health. It also mentions the development of vaccines and the political implications of the pandemic.
Scale, impact and risk
The pandemic resulted in millions of deaths worldwide and had significant global impacts, including changes in business and education practices. The disease is transmitted primarily through respiratory droplets and contaminated surfaces, and individuals at greatest risk include older adults and those with chronic illnesses.

The article provides general information about the response to the pandemic, including the use of vaccines, social distancing, and other preventive measures. It also notes the political aspects of the pandemic, such as the Senate committee's actions regarding Anthony Fauci.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

522,000 COVID-19 Cases 'Explode' in China in One Month... Concerns Rising in Korea - 아시아경제

News article (Korean original; verified English title unavailable)
新闻简报The news report highlights a significant surge in COVID-19 cases in China, with 522,000 reported from July 1 to 31, 2026, and a rising detection rate in South Korea. The dominant strain in China is the Omicron variant NB.1.8.1, while South Korea is primarily affected by BA.3.2. In China, the positivity rate among patients with influenza-like symptoms increased from 8.2% at the end of June to 21.2% by the end of July, indicating a potential resurgence. South Korea's detection rate for respiratory specimens rose to 9.1% in the third week of August, nearly 2.8 times higher than three weeks prior. Health authorities in South Korea are on high alert, and experts stress the importance of vaccine supply management and personal hygiene. The NB.1.8.1 variant has also been detected in South Korea, but the main strain remains BA.3.2. The report notes challenges in securing additional vaccine doses during outbreaks, citing recent shortages in Taiwan as an example. These developments underscore the need for continued monitoring and preparedness.

时间
2026年7月1日至31日,更新至2026年8月23日
地点与对象
中国与韩国。中国涉及1,041家监测样本医院,韩国涉及106家医疗机构
事件
A surge in COVID-19 cases in China and rising detection rates in South Korea, with the NB.1.8.1 variant dominant in China and BA.3.2 circulating in South Korea.
规模、影响与风险
2026年7月1日至31日中国报告522,000例病例,其中487例重症,1例死亡。中国出现流感样症状患者阳性率从8.2%升至21.2%;韩国检出率升至9.1%,较三周前增长近2.8倍
应对、状态与不确定性
韩国卫生当局已高度戒备,专家强调需主动加强抗病毒药物供应管理并严格落实个人卫生措施。NB.1.8.1变异株已在韩国检出,但主要流行株仍为BA.3.2。抗病毒药物生产与供应面临挑战,部分地区(如台湾)近期出现短缺

Original title (Korean)
522,000 COVID-19 Cases 'Explode' in China in One Month... Concerns Rising in Korea - 아시아경제
News Brief
The news report highlights a significant surge in COVID-19 cases in China, with 522,000 reported from July 1 to 31, 2026, and a rising detection rate in South Korea. The dominant strain in China is the Omicron variant NB.1.8.1, while South Korea is primarily affected by BA.3.2. In China, the positivity rate among patients with influenza-like symptoms increased from 8.2% at the end of June to 21.2% by the end of July, indicating a potential resurgence. South Korea's detection rate for respiratory specimens rose to 9.1% in the third week of August, nearly 2.8 times higher than three weeks prior. Health authorities in South Korea are on high alert, and experts stress the importance of vaccine supply management and personal hygiene. The NB.1.8.1 variant has also been detected in South Korea, but the main strain remains BA.3.2. The report notes challenges in securing additional vaccine doses during outbreaks, citing recent shortages in Taiwan as an example. These developments underscore the need for continued monitoring and preparedness.

container The Asia Business Daily Open Navigation Stock Economy Real estate Industry · IT Politics Society World Culture·Life Opinion·Editorial Visual-News Society "Another Wave Begins": 1 in 5 Suspected Patients Test Positive... 522,000 Cases in China, Sharp Surge in Korea in 3 Weeks Language Language Close KR CN Open Search bar Search field News Search Remove icon Search icon Dim Society Social General "Another Wave Begins": 1 in 5 Suspected Patients Test Positive... 522,000 Cases in China, Sharp Surge in Korea in 3 Weeks by Koo Nari Published 23 Aug.2026 15:03(KST) Updated 23 Aug.2026 17:47(KST) open/close Share Language Text Size Print Detection Rate and Hospitalizations on the Rise in Korea Health Authorities Remain on High Alert As signs of a COVID-19 resurgence emerge in China, health authorities and the medical community in Korea are on high alert. Getty Images Bank View original image According to surveillance data recently released by the Chinese Center for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), there were 522,000 reported cases of COVID-19 in China from July 1 to 31. Among these, 487 were severe cases, and there was one death. The dominant strain currently spreading in China is the Omicron variant NB.1.8.1. View original image The transmission rate is also high among patients with respiratory symptoms. The COVID-19 positivity rate among patients with influenza-like symptoms who visited the outpatient and emergency rooms of 1,041 sample surveillance hospitals across China surged from 8.2% at the end of June to 21.2% at the end of July. This means that one out of every five suspected patients who visited hospitals for COVID-19 or related symptoms tested positive. A more concerning issue is that the possibility of a COVID-19 resurgence is also increasing in Korea. According to respiratory virus surveillance data for the 33rd week of 2026 from the Korea Disease Control and Prevention Agency, the COVID-19 detection rate for respiratory specimens collected from 106 medical institutions nationwide between August 9 and 15 was 9.1%. This is nearly 2.8 times higher than the 3.2% reported three weeks earlier in the 30th week. Even compared to the previous week (32nd week) at 5.7%, the increase is notable. The number of hospitalized patients at tertiary general hospitals rose from two to seven. Although the scale remains small, experts note that both the rising detection rate and increased hospitalizations warrant close monitoring. However, although the NB.1.8.1 variant, which accounts for 99% of cases in China, has also been detected in Korea, the main strain currently circulating domestically is known to be BA.3.2. Meanwhile, experts have stressed the importance of proactive vaccine supply management and compliance with personal hygiene practices, given that it is difficult to predict the scale of outbreaks and referring to cases last year where vaccines ran out early in the vaccination campaign, preventing timely vaccinations for seniors and other high-risk groups. In particular, as vaccine production and supply take time, it is challenging to secure additional doses once an outbreak is in full swing. In fact, Taiwan recently experienced a temporary shortage of vaccines in some regions due to a rapid surge in cases. Hot Picks Today Trump Made 1,000 Stock Trades in a Month... COVID-19 Resurges in China With 520,000 Cases in July; Korea's Detection Rate Triples - Seoul Economic Daily

COVID-19 Resurges in China With 520,000 Cases in July; Korea's Detection Rate Triples - Seoul Economic Daily
新闻简报China reported 522,000 confirmed COVID-19 cases in July 2026, with the Omicron subvariant NB.1.8.1 accounting for 99% of sequenced samples, while sentinel hospitals recorded a 21.2% test positivity rate among outpatients with respiratory symptoms by late July. South Korea’s COVID-19 detection rate tripled over three weeks, rising from 3.2% in late July to 9.1% in mid-August, amid regional spread. Authorities in both countries note the current wave is milder than earlier pandemic waves, but warn of possible summer resurgence and localized vaccine shortages. South Korea has secured 4.84 million vaccine doses and approved a new variant-targeting vaccine, prioritizing older adults and high-risk groups for free immunization.
查看新闻五要素
时间
事件时间:2026年7月1日至31日(中国);2026年第30至33周(韩国)。发布日期:2026年8月22日。
地点与对象
中国全国;哨点医院(1,041个站点);韩国全国;医疗机构(106个站点);台湾(疫苗供应受影响)。
事件
Reported resurgence of COVID-19 driven by the Omicron subvariant NB.1.8.1 in China, with rising detection rates and hospitalizations in South Korea; media report.
规模、影响与风险
China: 522,000 confirmed cases in July; 487 severe cases; 1 death; positivity among respiratory outpatients rose to 21.2% by late July. South Korea: COVID-19 detection rate tripled from 3.2% (week 30) to 9.1% (week 33), a 2.8-fold increase in three weeks. Explicit risk: potential summer resurgence and vaccine supply disruption.
应对、状态与不确定性
China CDC surveillance ongoing; Chinese health authorities state current wave is not a large-scale pandemic; most infections mild; slowing in some regions. South Korea KDCA monitoring; national immunization program preparing 4.84 million doses; new variant-targeting vaccine approved; prioritized free vaccination for ≥65 years, high-risk facility residents, and immunocompromised people. Uncertainty: outbreak trajectory and vaccine demand timing remain difficult to predict.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

COVID-19 Resurges in China With 520,000 Cases in July; Korea's Detection Rate Triples - Seoul Economic Daily

News Brief
China reported 522,000 confirmed COVID-19 cases in July 2026, with the Omicron subvariant NB.1.8.1 accounting for 99% of sequenced samples, while sentinel hospitals recorded a 21.2% test positivity rate among outpatients with respiratory symptoms by late July. South Korea’s COVID-19 detection rate tripled over three weeks, rising from 3.2% in late July to 9.1% in mid-August, amid regional spread. Authorities in both countries note the current wave is milder than earlier pandemic waves, but warn of possible summer resurgence and localized vaccine shortages. South Korea has secured 4.84 million vaccine doses and approved a new variant-targeting vaccine, prioritizing older adults and high-risk groups for free immunization.
