Crowds at a market in Guangdong Province, China, in 2023. AFP/Yonhap COVID-19 is spreading again in China. More than 520,000 confirmed cases were reported in July, and the positivity rate among patients visiting hospitals with respiratory symptoms rose for a fifth straight week to surpass 20%. Detection rates and hospitalizations are also rising in South Korea, heightening concerns over a possible summer resurgence. 520,000 Cases in a Month; 21.2% Positivity Among Respiratory Patients According to the latest surveillance data released by the Chinese Center for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), 522,000 new COVID-19 cases were reported in China from July 1 to 31. Of these, 487 were severe cases and one was a death. Detection of COVID-19 also rose rapidly among patients with respiratory symptoms. Across 1,041 sentinel surveillance hospitals nationwide, the positivity rate among outpatient and emergency room patients with influenza-like symptoms climbed from 8.2% during June 29 to July 5 to 11.2%, 15.3% and 19.5%, before reaching 21.2% during July 27 to August 2. That marks a 13 percentage point increase over five weeks. The current wave in China is driven by the Omicron subvariant NB.1.8.1. All 11,204 genome samples from domestic infections secured by the Chinese CDC in July were Omicron variants, and NB.1.8.1 and its sublineages accounted for 99.0% based on the most recent week. However, Chinese health authorities do not view the current situation as a large-scale spread on the scale of the past pandemic. The recent increase has slowed, with some regions showing a decline, and most infections have been found to be mild. Korea's Detection Rate Triples in Three Weeks Amid Regional Spread China's spread draws attention because Korea's own COVID-19 indicators are rising at the same time. According to the Korea Disease Control and Prevention Agency's respiratory virus surveillance data for the 33rd week of 2026, the COVID-19 detection rate in respiratory samples collected from 106 medical institutions nationwide from August 9 to 15 stood at 9.1%. That was up 3.4 percentage points from 5.7% in the previous 32nd week. The upward trend is even clearer in recent movements. The COVID-19 detection rate stood at 3.2% in the 30th week, 5.9% in the 31st week and 5.7% in the 32nd week before rising to 9.1% in the 33rd week. Compared with three weeks earlier, that is a roughly 2.8-fold increase. The overall respiratory virus detection rate also reached 59.7% in the 33rd week. Aside from COVID-19, parainfluenza virus was highest at 20.5%, followed by rhinovirus at 14.8% and influenza virus at 5.4%. In Taiwan, a recent rise in COVID-19 patients has led to temporary vaccine shortages in some areas. With the spread continuing in neighboring countries such as China and Taiwan and cross-border travel remaining active, some point to the need to closely watch domestic trends. "Prevent Vaccine Supply Disruptions Early in the Rollout" Industry officials stress that managing vaccine supply early in the rollout is important because the scale of a COVID-19 outbreak is difficult to predict accurately. Demand could concentrate particularly in the first one to two weeks when flu and COVID-19 vaccinations begin together. Last year, too, some medical institutions ran out of COVID-19 vaccines faster than expected early in the rollout, leaving older adults and others unable to get vaccinated in time. Because vaccines take time to produce and distribute, there are limits to securing additional supplies once an outbreak is in full swing. The government has secured 4.84 million doses of COVID-19 vaccine this year. Moderna's new variant-targeting vaccine, Spikevax Prefilled Syringe, also received product approval from the Ministry of Food and Drug Safety on the 6th. Under the national immunization program, free vaccinations will be provided to older adults aged 65 and over, patients and residents at infection-vulnerable facilities, and immunocompromised people. An industry official said, "Older adults often do not return to medical institutions once they miss their vaccination window," adding, "Minimizing supply disruptions early in the rollout is more important than anything else."
